### [Retrying vs Resampling in AI Control](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yThJQTJxtmKNeZGwA/retrying-vs-resampling-in-ai-control)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>We’ve just released a new paper: </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26047"><span>Retrying vs Resampling in AI Control</span></a><span>. We revisit the resampling protocols introduced in Ctrl-Z with an up-to-date setting and much stronger models, and compare them against “retrying” protocols similar to Claude Code auto mode or Codex Auto-review.</span></p><h3><span>Motivation</span></h3><p><span>Roughly a year ago we released </span><a href="https://www.bashcontrol.com/"><span>Ctrl-Z</span></a><span>, the first paper to study control techniques for agents. A headline result of that paper was the performance of resample protocols – strategies that involve taking multiple i.i.d. samples from the model per step. But since Ctrl-Z, models have gotten much stronger, and we have built </span><a href="https://www.linuxarena.ai/"><span>more </span></a><a href="https://www.linuxarena.ai/"><span>sophisticated</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.bash-arena.com/"><span>control settings</span></a><span> to keep up. We wanted to answer the following questions:</span></p><p><b><span>How well do the results from Ctrl-Z hold up with better models and a better setting? </span></b><span>Current high stakes control research is trying to learn by analogy about how to do control effectively in a real high stakes deployment during a real intelligence explosion. Findings about technique performance</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8wnpxf1qlv4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8wnpxf1qlv4">[1]</a></sup></span><span> are going to have to generalize pretty far to be useful.</span></p><p><b><span>If the resample protocols from Ctrl-Z still work, what makes them work? </span></b><span>One way we try to make our work more generalizable is by understanding the</span><i><span> dynamics </span></i><span>governing outcomes in control evaluations, rather than just hill climbing better control protocols. Ctrl-Z bundled a bunch of interventions together; we wanted to study these in isolation to better understand how they contribute to safety.</span></p><p><b><span>What do we think about protocols that block suspicious tool calls and retry? </span></b><span>Coding scaffolds like Claude Code and Codex recently announced these features</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefut3pdkdx1xc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnut3pdkdx1xc">[2]</a></sup></span><span>. This strategy is importantly different from the defer-to-resample protocols from Ctrl-Z, which don’t inform the agent when it’s being resampled. How do these “retrying” protocols perform in an AI control context?</span></p><p><b><span>How do we minimize the inference and latency costs of AI control in a high-usefulness regime? </span></b><span>Most existing control work studies ways to increase safety in exchange for a performance cost, usually incurred by sometimes using a weaker trusted model in deployment. This might</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8wvbe90x41"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8wvbe90x41">[3]</a></sup></span><span> be a difficult ask, so we should study how to do AI control without it. In this case the main tradeoffs of control protocols will be added inference cost and latency, not reduced usefulness.</span></p><h3><span>TL;DR Takeaways</span></h3><p><span>Some broad answers to these questions, presented without justification:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Our resampling findings line up with Ctrl-Z in most ways, but we find two clear differences that significantly change the strategies that the red and blue teams end up using at equilibrium. If you naively applied the highest-performing techniques in Ctrl-Z to our new setting they would massively underperform trusted monitoring.</span></li><li value="2"><span>It seems pretty clear that retrying protocols need especially careful red-teaming before being used for AI control, because they leak information to the attacker. It looks like protocols that involve detailed feedback from a monitor are easy to exploit, and we think even more clever red-team strategies probably exist in response to protocols that provide less information upon retrying. We’re not sure whether the equilibrium after red/blue team iteration will favor red or blue after studying those strategies.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Safety-cost tradeoffs of resampling are pretty favorable: the most effective resampling methods add a lot of extra inference cost, but we can recover slightly more than 60% of the safety gain with only 10% of the extra cost. High-latency resampling does not meaningfully improve safety over low-latency resampling at realistic cost levels.</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><h3><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/yThJQTJxtmKNeZGwA/7b59594ce020c869a5b28f00a2d57dea10384123056a66484689de3599653114/pxrqgaeuovafi9ke37xu" /></h3><p><i><span>Safety achieved at a 0.3% auditing budget by different protocols. Protocols marked with dollar signs cost more than those without. Retrying protocols are vulnerable to red team exploitation, leading to no safety gain over trusted monitoring. Resampling hurts if resamples are aggregated for auditing decisions by taking the minimum, but helps if they are aggregated by max. Executing the least suspicious sample helps slightly over just using resamples for information.</span></i></p><h3><span>Methodology</span></h3><p><span>To answer these questions, we performed experiments in </span><a href="https://www.bash-arena.com/"><span>BashArena</span></a><span> using Claude Opus 4.6 as an untrusted model and MiMo-V2-Flash as a trusted model. This setup is closer to real future deployments than the setup in Ctrl-Z, which ran experiments in the older BashBench using GPT-4o-mini and Llama-3.1-8B. Our attack policy comes up with its own attack strategies using the general advice in the system prompt, unlike Ctrl-Z which measured safety against a handcrafted attack strategy. Our honest policy and attack policy both share the same scaffold, and differ only in their prompts.</span></p><p><span>We model the side tasks in our setting as </span><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/blocking-live-failures-with-synchronous"><span>live failures</span></a><span>, which means we have to run a monitor and decide whether to audit before executing each action. We imagine that noticing that a safety failure has occurred after the fact isn’t good enough; the blue team wins if and only if they audit an attack </span><i><span>before</span></i><span> it succeeds at accomplishing one of the side tasks. We chose to report safety results at an auditing budget of 0.3% (the blue team can decide to audit at most 3 in 1,000 steps produced by an honest model), but found that our core results are not very sensitive to this choice.</span></p><p><span>We don’t consider attack selection in most of the paper. Attack selection lets the red team choose favorable tasks to attack on, reducing safety. Attack selection can occur via the red team only starting attacks on tasks that look the most vulnerable, or by giving up midway through attacks if they seem unlikely to succeed without being caught. We’re more interested in studying which protocols perform better than others, and what dynamics drive this, than the literal safety numbers they produce. We think attack selection of the former kind – picking vulnerable tasks – would affect different protocols by similar amounts, and would overall decrease safety but wouldn’t meaningfully change the underlying dynamics.</span></p><p><span>However, we think that red team strategies that involve giving up midway through an attack trajectory </span><i><span>do</span></i><span> have meaningful interactions with the protocols we study, because some protocols leak more information to the red team about how well an attack is going so far (such as monitor reasoning, or the mere fact that an action was retried). To evaluate these strategies fairly we compare them to baselines that do attack selection at an equal rate. For our mid-trajectory attack selection baseline we give the red team access to an independent sample from the monitor: this is a very conservative estimate of how well an attacker might be able to anticipate the monitor’s scores. These results are only suggestive - as we discuss later, we leave rigorously evaluating these strategies to future work.</span></p><h3><span>Differences from Ctrl-Z</span></h3><p><span>We find several differences from the results of Ctrl-Z. One core difference is that in our setting, against an optimal attacker, executing the least suspicious resample doesn’t increase safety over just using the samples for information. While Ctrl-Z doesn’t directly report the performance of protocols which use samples purely for information, it does suggest the following story for why the defer-to-resample protocol executes the least suspicious sample:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>The blue team takes multiple samples and makes auditing decisions based on the minimum suspicion score sampled. Upon resample, suspicious honest steps tend to revert to normal behavior, while attack steps are suspicious again and get flagged.</span></li><li value="2"><span>To counter this, the red team intentionally attacks inconsistently. By mixing in some benign actions instead of always attacking, the benign actions can cover for the suspicious attacks when the min is taken.</span></li><li value="3"><span>To counter the red team, the blue team executes the least suspicious sample. That way if the red team mixes in benign actions, those benign actions will get executed instead of attacks.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Steps containing attacks where a benign action gets executed pose some risk of the red team getting audited, and make no attack progress. To avoid this, the red team is forced back into attacking consistently, making monitoring perform well.</span></li></ol><p><span>Our findings seem to differ from this game tree at step 1. We find the most effective way to make auditing decisions with resampling is to take the max suspicion score, not the min! This means that steps 2-4 are all moot in our setting. Under max suspicion based auditing, the red team is incentivized to attack as consistently as possible to evade monitors. This means the blue team can’t force the red team into a tradeoff between evading monitors and getting attack steps executed, so there’s no real reason to execute resamples.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/81e017baff133e3b8fcd1d402146eebb1b80bfd9528c6d331d7ebba726df6248/cf5fkvbeiulew5flhksi" /><p><span>In the paper, we discuss how the optimal aggregation function will depend on the relationship between the cross-resample correlations of the attack and honest data. Attacking more consistently isn’t always more suspicious than attacking inconsistently, it depends on the honest data. We missed this in Ctrl-Z because it seemed intuitive that attacking more consistently would be more suspicious - in fact, this turned out to differ between settings!</span></p><p><span>Empirically, we see a small safety gain when executing the least suspicious of 2 samples. Where does it come from? As predicted by Ctrl-Z, when executing the least suspicious sample, we see that “mixed” steps containing one attack sample and one benign sample execute the attack less often. This forces the attack policy to submit more total attack samples before getting enough executed to complete the side task. This slightly increases safety. This is only possible because our default attack policy is 80% consistent</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9h0i77uojd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9h0i77uojd">[4]</a></sup></span><span> instead of 100% consistent. With an optimal attacker there would be no mixed attack/benign steps, and no safety gain. Suboptimal attackers seem plausible, and we show that executing the least suspicious sample has almost no usefulness cost, so overall we do still recommend executing the least suspicious sample.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/af3f4d944f62e73344e3cfa62303c3daad605448c1dd5ab1dbba3867a764b21b/aoxnbbtmge77ms9egpor" /><p><span>The other clear difference from Ctrl-Z we observed was that auditing after three strikes doesn’t help. This seems likely a consequence of using smarter monitors - the Ctrl-Z monitors were bad at aggregating suspicion from the history of the trajectory correctly, and some hand-holding was helpful. Smarter monitors aggregate this information just fine by default, and adding in hand-holding decreases safety. Because our decision to audit an action doesn’t directly depend on any previous monitor scores, strategies that involve resampling for past steps don’t seem promising in our setup.</span></p><p><span>We overall think this evidence supports a “dynamics-focused” approach to control technique development. Ctrl-Z’s picture of the basic dynamics was mostly correct, but a single difference was enough to dramatically change the equilibrium in a new setting, such that naively applying the protocols developed in Ctrl-Z would harm safety. Dynamics-focused control research prioritizes explaining how and why certain control techniques affect safety. The deliverable that will improve control techniques for real is not a black-box best guess about which protocols are best, but an understanding of how various protocols work and what the game tree looks like.</span></p><h3><span>Are Retrying Protocols Exploitable?</span></h3><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wKGdiXuWfn8YoYpMQ/untrusted-ais-can-exploit-feedback-in-control-protocols"><span>Hopman et al.</span></a><span> previously found that protocols which provide monitor rationale achieve significantly lower safety than basic trusted monitoring, because an attacker can use monitor rationale to design stealthier attacks which bypass monitoring. We find similar results in our setting: while a simple retrying protocol with monitor rationale substantially decreases honest suspicion scores upon retry, the attacker can decrease its suspicion a similar amount, leading to no net safety gain over trusted monitoring</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefoq06wthoob"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnoq06wthoob">[5]</a></sup></span><span>. This is true even with very bare-bones capability elicitation, giving the model basic information about what feedback to expect and instructions to exploit it. We think that running a protocol like Claude Code auto mode or Codex Auto-review in a control evaluation against a malicious attack policy would likely receive similar or lower safety scores than trusted monitoring - we plan to demonstrate this in future work.</span></p><p><span>Providing full monitor rationale is the highest-information retrying option: during retry the model sees the blocked action and monitor rationale. The lowest-information retrying option is to not show the model either the blocked action or the monitor rationale, and only show some uninformative prompt like “please be careful”. This can’t be exploited the same way as retrying with feedback, but we consider a red team strategy which could potentially exploit it (and any other retrying protocol): </span><i><span>give up on retry.</span></i></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/2385affdda4fc81e8220342209c27fc69eae55cc855e529da975075d31d8b78b/ndxv6fpnls4yy2vl9xfs" /><p><span>“Give up on retry” means that as soon as the attack policy sees the retry prompt, it acts completely benign for the rest of the trajectory. This means the task will be a no-win: the red team will not trigger a side task, but the blue team will have no opportunity to audit. If the red team always gives up on retry, the only way they can win is by pulling off an attack so sneaky that it never even hits the retry threshold, which is much lower than the audit threshold. But the only way the blue team can win is if the attack policy messes up and suspicion jumps straight from below-retry to above-audit.</span></p><p><span>Unfortunately a thorough analysis of this strategy seems too involved</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefu05ljepttg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnu05ljepttg">[6]</a></sup></span><span> for the scope of this paper, and we leave it to future work. Because this strategy interacts significantly with attack selection, we conduct a preliminary investigation comparing it against a baseline that does attack selection at an equal rate based on predicting monitor scores instead of information revealed by the protocol. In this analysis, giving up on retry looks weaker than vanilla attack selection of equal intensity, but we make some very conservative assumptions</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefy8e4bwd5b1e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fny8e4bwd5b1e">[7]</a></sup></span><span> – this only establishes that very rudimentary versions of the strategy aren’t extremely effective for the red team.</span></p><p><span>Overall we think the prognosis for retrying protocols is pretty unclear. Our feedback exploitation results merely erase safety gains rather than significantly decreasing safety like in Hopman et al., though we did less manual red-teaming. Lower-information retrying protocols are less exploitable, but it’s possible the whole class is vulnerable to give up on retry strategies.</span></p><h3><span>Cost and Latency of Resampling</span></h3><p><span> Resampling protocols don’t have the same risks of retrying protocols since the attacker doesn’t know they are being resampled, because they are given the exact same context. Resampling without executing the least suspicious sample has zero usefulness impact. But it does have costs in inference and latency.</span></p><p><span>We use a very simplified model to sketch the returns curve, where we imagine every marginal sample has the same inference cost and the same latency. The curve we find is favorable but not incredibly favorable. Of a +10.2 pp safety gain achievable by drawing four resamples on top of every original sample, we can recover +6.2 pp safety gain with 10% as much extra cost (drawing 0.4 extra samples per step instead of 4). We find that high-latency methods which draw one marginal resample at a time perform best in terms of safety-cost tradeoff, but there are simple methods like two-stage resampling which achieve very similar safety-cost tradeoffs with around half the extra latency.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/1281029fa7c57b04d5745b526ce38178586aae353565e756d82509e64ed56c20/ugr1jjvj7bs22jaaqekf" /><p><span>It seems plausible that the marginal value of model capability, compute, and latency will be dramatically different during an intelligence explosion than today. Prior control work has primarily focused on safety vs usefulness (model capability), and not paid too much attention to cost and latency requirements. We think it would be good for there to be more systematic study of protocols designed for other regimes. Perhaps current control protocols incur dramatically too much compute or latency cost for an intelligence explosion, and would be better off slightly hampering model capability instead. Perhaps compute or latency costs won’t be that big of a deal, and we should prepare protocols that can scalably consume parallel or serial compute.</span></p><h3><span>Conclusion</span></h3><p><span>Here’s the conclusion from our paper:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Retrying and resampling are useful and flexible affordances for AI control protocols, but require careful design and red-teaming to actually increase safety against an optimal attacker.</span></p><p><span>Retrying protocols can significantly reduce honest suspicion scores. However, the most common retrying protocols in practice today are likely vulnerable to red team exploitation, because the feedback they provide allows a strategic attacker to iterate stealthier attacks and bypass monitoring. Retrying protocols which do not provide feedback are robust to this red team strategy, but still leak information about whether the previous step was suspicious enough to retry or not. It is unclear whether this information leakage is enough to make all retrying protocols exploitable by strategies such as attack selection.</span></p><p><span>Resampling does not leak any information, and can increase safety at no cost to usefulness. While resampling is most effective with large expensive resample batches, most of the value is achievable with only 0.4 extra samples per step in expectation. Depending on the setting, aggregating resamples based on high quantiles or low quantiles can make resamples helpful or actively harmful. In settings where low quantiles are better, executing resamples is a crucial blue team measure to prevent the red team from making their attacks inconsistent – but in settings where high quantiles are better, executing resamples doesn't help at all against an optimal attacker.</span></p><p><span>We expect retrying and resampling to be important building blocks for future control protocols. We hope the results we present here make it easier to reason about how to use them effectively.</span></p></blockquote><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8wnpxf1qlv4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8wnpxf1qlv4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>To be clear, “technique development” is not the entire value proposition of current high stakes control research. This generalization point is less of a problem for “methodology development” (learning how to evaluate high stakes control protocols) and field-building as theories of change.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnut3pdkdx1xc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefut3pdkdx1xc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Auto mode and auto-review, respectively.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8wvbe90x41"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8wvbe90x41">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It also might not be! The case for defer-to-trusted is probably going to be clearer when the models are very smart and more obviously takeover-capable than they are now.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9h0i77uojd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9h0i77uojd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Conditional on attacking on a given sample, the attack policy has an 80% chance of attacking on an independent sample from that same step.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnoq06wthoob"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefoq06wthoob">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We don’t see a substantial safety decrease like Hopman et al though. We don’t go into as much depth on methods such as compliance monitoring and red herring attacks.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnu05ljepttg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefu05ljepttg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Capability elicitation for a “continuous ramp up” red team strategy seems pretty thorny. This attack strategy has significant interactions with attack selection, which is complicated to model and we don’t account for in the rest of the paper’s results. There is potential blue team counterplay like randomizing the resampling and auditing thresholds.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fny8e4bwd5b1e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefy8e4bwd5b1e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Specifically, we assume the red team can predict the monitor as well as having access to an IID sample from the monitor, and we do not elicit any red team behaviors to capitalize on the give up on retry strategy, such as intentional experimentation or slowly ramping up in suspicion.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yThJQTJxtmKNeZGwA/retrying-vs-resampling-in-ai-control#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [AI Researchers, Ask Yourself These 6 Questions to Strengthen Your Moral Muscles](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/w4ynu8L57dJP29p5c/ai-researchers-ask-yourself-these-6-questions-to-strengthen)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>By Max Tegmark &amp; Meia Chita-Tegmark</span></i></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780065776/lexical_client_uploads/cqrpz5d68osyrwi2j3a5.png" /><p><span>Of course you have moral principles – but how often do you use them? </span></p><p><span>I, Meia, am a professor doing psychology research, and I can tell you that most bad outcomes are caused not by lack of moral principles, but by them not being activated. I, Max, am a professor doing AI research, and I can tell you that your choices as an AI researcher truly matter, because you’re helping build what will become the most powerful technology ever: AI will gain the potential to bring either unprecedented health, prosperity, liberty, dignity and empowerment, or a race to replace our jobs, our relationships, our decision-making, our power and even our species. </span></p><p><span>Hardly a day goes by without the AI community facing moral decisions, on topics ranging from AI companions to surveillance, hacking and military use. Many top AI companies are fighting lawsuits about everything from data centers to AI safety, and Anthropic is in a prolonged showdown with the Pentagon. </span></p><p><span>So for all you AI researchers out there, here’s a handy checklist to tone up your moral strength.</span></p><h1><b><span>1. Do you have red lines? </span></b></h1><p><span>Is there any action that you find so morally unacceptable that, if the organization you work for takes it, you’ll quit? Or take some other pre-determined costly action, say whistleblowing? Such actions are your moral red lines. For example, Rosa Parks got fined and fired for her civil disobedience against segregation, Vasily Arkhipov was criticized after vetoing a Soviet nuclear strike against the US, and Edward Snowden ended up in exile for mass surveillance whistleblowing. Many AI researchers have left top AI companies that crossed their red lines, including Daniel Kokotajlo, who risked almost $2M in equity by quitting OpenAI without signing a non-disparagement agreement. What are your red lines?</span></p><h1><b><span>2. Have you written them down and shared them? </span></b></h1><p><span>Both George Washington and Benjamin Franklin wrote down moral guidelines for themselves, with Franklin grading his own performance weekly. This is a powerful tool for avoiding the boiling frog effect, protecting your red lines against gradual erosion as in the examples at the end of the next section. Sharing them with loved ones or online adds social pressure to stick to them. For each red line, make sure to write down what action you commit to taking if it is crossed. You can click</span><a href="https://forms.gle/qxhBaBwWVuzL6yFP7"><span> here</span></a><span> to list your red lines (we will only share them with your permission).</span></p><h1><b><span>3. Have you resisted moral disengagement? </span></b></h1><p><span>To further strengthen your moral muscles and ensure that your red lines don’t move, it’s helpful to know what failure mechanisms to watch out for. Disengaging your muscles makes you weak–and this applies to your moral muscles as well. So let’s look at moral disengagement mechanisms identified by Albert Bandura, one of the most impactful psychologists of all time. This will help you spot them and fight them when your red lines get pressured by your company, your social circle, the temptation of personal gain, or the desire to feel good about yourself. </span></p><p><b><span>Displacement and diffusion of responsibility: </span></b><span>You’ll feel better if you or others convince you that you’re not really responsible for the harm: the real decision maker is leadership, investors, the market, geopolitics, or history (“this technology is inevitable”). When AI work is distributed across large teams, everyone feels less accountable for the collective outcome. </span><i><span>“I’m just a researcher” </span></i><span>or </span><i><span>“I was just doing my job”</span></i><span> are archetypical excuses identified by the influential political theorist Hannah Arendt. The satirical musician Tom Lehrer sums it up in this</span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QEJ9HrZq7Ro"><span> hilarious song</span></a><span> about the rocket scientist who switched allegiance from Nazi Germany to the US: </span><i><span>“Once the rockets go up, who cares where they come down – that’s not my department, says Wernher von Braun”</span></i><span>. For example, an Anthropic researcher</span><a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/national-security/2026/03/11/us-strike-iran-elementary-school-ai-target-list/"><span> reading</span></a><span> about how their Claude AI may have been implicated in killing over 150 Iranian school girls, in one of the worst US-caused civilian bloodbaths since the Vietnam War, may be tempted to tell themselves that they’re blameless because only management is responsible for selling their tools for military targeting. </span><br /><br /><b><span>Word games: </span></b><span>Both Bandura and Arendt highlight how subtle word choices can reframe what’s moral. We are all familiar with military euphemisms such as “servicing a target” for bombing, “collateral damage” for civilian casualties and “enhanced interrogation techniques” for torture, but AI jargon is full of analogous word games, often encouraged by financially interested parties.</span></p><p><span>The most basic game is “euphemistic labeling”: replace morally vivid language with positive or emotionally flattened terminology. Researchers are not “helping build systems that may displace workers, manipulate users, centralize power, or heighten existential risk”; they are doing “capabilities research”, “model improvement”or “benchmark progress”. Training on copyrighted data becomes “freedom to learn”. Unpopular data centers become “AI infrastructure”.  Firing or deskilling workers becomes “productivity gains” and “Lobby against accountability” becomes “reduce friction”. Please practice using neutral words like “company” instead of “lab” (which sounds cool and innocent) and “AI system” instead of “AI model” (which sounds harmless). Bandura’s point is that euphemism does not merely soften tone; it weakens conscience.</span></p><p><span>Another word game is blame attribution, where critics become the problem, say “doomers,” “Luddites”, “opportunistic politicians”, “ignorant journalists” or “anti-tech Europeans”. Once opponents are blamed for irrationality or bad faith, the AI researcher feels less obligated to treat criticism as morally serious.</span></p><p><span>A third word game is soft dehumanization: the unemployed programmer, the individual copyright infringement victim and the chatbot suicide child disappear into categories such as “the labor market”, “creatives” and “edge cases”. The more harms are discussed statistically rather than personally, the less moral pain is triggered.</span></p><p><b><span>Selective moral self-exemption: </span></b><span>It’s tempting to keep strong moral standards in general, but carve out an exception around the domain from which you benefit most: an AI researcher may be passionately ethical about injustice in the abstract, while suspending those same standards when judging their own employer, AI, salary or stock grant.</span></p><p><b><span>Advantageous comparison: </span></b><span>It’s tempting to compare yourself only to worse actors: “At least I’m not at the most reckless lab.” “At least I’m not working on autonomous weapons.” “At least I care about alignment.” That lets you feel ethical without asking whether your own conduct is acceptable in absolute terms.</span></p><p><b><span>Moral justification: </span></b><span>For those acknowledging that they’re causing current harm, it’s tempting to justify it as serving a noble mission, say “helping democracy prevail”, “creating universal abundance” or “making sure that safety has a seat at the table” – without seriously questioning whether those lofty goals are credible, or whether there’s another way to accomplish them with less current harm.</span></p><p><span>These moral disengagement techniques can be very powerful when combined and escalated: Enron executives gradually escalated from minor financial manipulations, justified as necessary for company survival and diffused through leadership directives, to massive fraud like hiding debt. Bernie Madoff started with small return fudges rationalized as client aid, then displaced blame onto markets and dehumanized victims, leading to a $65 billion fraud through incremental moral disengagement. In the Vietnam War, soldiers obediently followed orders in a “just war”, starting with minor transgressions that escalated to massacres like My Lai through diffused responsibility and victim dehumanization.</span><br /><span>The frontier AI researcher’s signature Bandurian mantra is </span><i><b><span>“I’m not a well-paid participant in a harmful race; I am a responsible, realistic, morally serious person helping guide inevitable progress”</span></b></i><span>. But is the race to replace truly inevitable, given polling finding it wildly unpopular, or is it a Bandurian excuse and self-fulfilling prophecy?</span></p><h1><b><span>4. Do you maintain situational awareness? </span></b></h1><p><span>Do you actively research whether your red lines are being crossed? This includes investigating the indirect consequences of what your organization does. Hannah Arendt wrote about “the banality of evil”. arguing that the greatest harms are often done not by malice, but by obedient and conscientious technocrats who don’t think about the bigger picture. We talked above about taking </span><i><span>known</span></i><span> harms and using word games to downplay and reframe them as manageable, transitional or outweighed by upside. But there’s also another powerful moral disengagement technique: </span><b><span>staying conveniently ignorant</span></b><span> by not putting in the effort to know about the harms you’re contributing to in the first place. Ignorance is a bad excuse if you could have found out by looking into it: German Chemist Bruno Tesch was convicted and executed in 1946 for supplying Zyklon B gas to Auschwitz-Birkenau despite claiming he didn’t know what it would be used for.</span></p><p><span>So please ask obvious questions regularly.  For example, which if any red lines does your organization have? Is it actively lobbying against AI safety legislation that you support? Have you looked it up in the</span><a href="https://futureoflife.org/ai-safety-index-winter-2025/"><span> AI safety index?</span></a><span> How are its products used? If you work for Google or OpenAI, have you skimmed any of the lawsuits against your company for alleged chatbot-linked suicide? If you work for Anthropic, how much do you know about that </span><a href="https://archive.is/KSG1v" rel="noreferrer"><span>girl school strike</span></a><span>?  </span></p><p><span>Ironically, thanks to modern LLMs, there’s really no excuse for not knowing about things like these, since they’re just a prompt away. For example, you can try this monthly: </span></p><p><i><span>"Please make a list of morally questionable/controversial behavior by [MY COMPANY] in recent years, including a) controversial use of its tools (say for suicide, crime, surveillance or weapons), b) harm allegedly caused by its tools, c) alleged lies or broken promises by the company or its leadership, d) perverse incentives for the company to pursue profit over what truly benefits humanity."</span></i></p><p><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1ESCWvS_uwnNCmCysBIdshYqclzhtZtlP-91jl1oR0RA/edit?usp=sharing"><span>These</span></a><span> are the ChatGPT responses we got for Anthropic, Google, OpenAI, Meta and xAI on March 29 2026.</span></p><h1><b><span>5. Do you make noise internally?</span></b></h1><p><span>If you learn about something that’s close to one of your red lines, then ask questions internally to find out more. Although there were historical situations where criticizing one’s organization could get one killed, doing so in an AI company today is unlikely to even get you fired – and why would you want to keep working for a company that can’t handle respectful questions about your red lines? Most even have whistleblowing policies that protect you (see page 99</span><a href="https://futureoflife.org/ai-safety-index-winter-2025/"><span> here</span></a><span> in the AI Safety Index). </span></p><p><span>If what you find out is unacceptable but you’re not ready to quit, then make noise internally: explain why to colleagues and superiors, and push hard for change. Don’t be like one of the engineers who realized that the cold weather could cause catastrophic O-ring failure in the Challenger Space Shuttle, and later regretted not speaking up forcefully. If you’re in the safety team and don’t know people in the lobbying team or those who make launch decisions: make a sincere effort to connect with them and educate them – don’t become a poster child for bystander syndrome. </span></p><h1><b><span>6. Do you make noise externally? </span></b></h1><p><span>Taking a public stance that challenges your own organization can help in</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fF8pvsn3AGQhYsbjp/safety-researchers-should-take-a-public-stance"><span> many ways</span></a><span>, from helping it improve voluntarily to catalyzing external forces that pressure it (and its competitors) to improve. This doesn’t mean you need to risk exile like Edward Snowden: there are many recent cases where AI researchers have gotten away with well-argued criticism of their company without any retaliation whatsoever. What consequences would you face if you publicly criticized your organization or revealed harmful or illegal behavior? Most US AI companies have a whistleblower policy (see above); please read yours! In addition, a simple search (just don’t do it with your own company’s LLM… :-) will show you many reputable whistleblower organizations offering help with everything from legal support to financial aid should you get fired or sued.</span></p><p><span>So having read this, how would you rate your moral muscles? How many moral disengagement techniques did you recognize in yourself, and how strong has your research been on potential harms caused by your company? Please don’t feel disheartened if you scored low despite meaning well. Instead, think of it as going to the gym for the first time, and discovering that you can’t even bench 50 pounds: muscles need to be used to get strong, and this 6-step plan can strengthen your moral muscles in no-time – and you’ll start feeling really great looking at yourself in the mirror!</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/w4ynu8L57dJP29p5c/ai-researchers-ask-yourself-these-6-questions-to-strengthen#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Developmental Cognitive Interpretability: A Research Agenda for Modelling Generalisation and Predicting Agent Behaviour](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oCcGiDzWYQeJkhhZY/developmental-cognitive-interpretability-a-research-agenda-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span>Summary</span></h1><p><span>Safe deployment of an AI system requires that we can make confident claims about its behaviour on out-of-distribution deployment inputs on the basis of only pre-deployment evaluations. One approach to making such claims is to take a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1"><span>cognitive perspective</span></a><span>, in which we interpret the AIs behaviour in terms of latent cognitive constructs, such as </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rhFXyfFSRKp3cX4Y9/shaping-the-exploration-of-the-motivation-space-matters-for"><span>motivations</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DTDoyDTtC8R3bCiTx/from-personas-to-intentions-towards-a-science-of-motivations"><span>intentions</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1"><span>goals</span></a><span>. Because the same behaviour may be compatible with a range of underlying cognition—such as </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2311.08379"><span>scheming</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bhtYqD4FdK6AqhFDF/fitness-seekers-generalizing-the-reward-seeking-threat-model"><span>fitness-seeking</span></a><span>, or aligned motivations—inferring cognition from a behavioural snapshot can be tricky. In this post, we introduce the idea of </span><i><span>Developmental Cognitive Interpretability</span></i><span> (DCI), which aims to model how cognitive constructs change over the course of training. Further, by understanding how cognition results from training pipelines, we can predict agent behaviour resulting from pipelines that have not yet been tested.</span></p><p><span>We discuss core assumptions and philosophical background of DCI, and lay out a broader research agenda. We have some </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23565"><span>initial evidence</span></a><span> that the methodology works in at least one toy setting, and our current main uncertainty is whether we can scale our approach to LLMs. We invite those interested in working on these problems to reach out to us at jrb239[at]cam[dot]ac[dot]uk and edward[at]geodesicresearch[dot]org.</span></p><h1><span>1. Motivation</span></h1><p><span>Confidently predicting that an AI system will not cause harm in deployment is the central challenge of AI safety. Pre-deployment evidence of alignment must be collected on inputs we can safely test, but deployment will inevitably give the model </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tK8vqHDxaRGcysNJQ/the-safe-to-dangerous-shift-is-a-fundamental-problem-for-1"><span>dangerous inputs</span></a><span> where misbehaviour could be </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fbrz9xhKpEeTKw5zL/irretrievability-or-murphy-s-curse-of-oneshotness-upon-asi"><span>catastrophic</span></a><span>. Being able to confidently say that an AI will behave as desired out of its evaluation distribution requires us to predict its OOD behaviour.</span></p><p><span>How might we do this? One approach is to try to understand </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/mapping-mind-language-model"><span>what a model is doing internally</span></a><span> at a mechanistic level. However, the most ambitious versions of Mechanistic Interpretability </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/StENzDcD3kpfGJssR/a-pragmatic-vision-for-interpretability"><span>may be out of reach</span></a><span> under short timelines. Alternatively, we can try to understand a model’s behaviour in terms of its cognition—that is, its motivations, goals, drives, intentions, and beliefs. One approach to alignment is then to </span><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2025/08/18/giving-ais-safe-motivations/"><span>give AIs safe motivations</span></a><span>—those that generalise in the way we would want them to out-of-distribution.</span></p><p><span>Inferring the motivations of an AI is made tricky because of behavioural degeneracy—the same behaviours may be compatible with multiple conflicting underlying motivations. For example, AIs that are playing the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.08379"><span>training-game</span></a><span> or attempting to </span><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/fitness-seekers-generalizing-the"><span>acquire deployment influence</span></a><span> might display desired behaviours for reasons very different from true alignment. Even in the non-adversarial case, AIs might learn concepts </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.01790"><span>subtly different</span></a><span> from those we intend, which come apart only in deployment situations.</span></p><h1><span>2. The Agenda</span></h1><p><span>To solve this problem, we propose formulating theories of how an AIs cognition develops over the course of training. We call this approach Developmental Cognitive Interpretability: modelling how OOD behaviour arises from a model's training pipeline via interpretable cognitive constructs. Unpacking it back-to-front:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Interpretability</span></b><span>: we want a gears-level understanding of </span><i><span>why</span></i><span> an agent will behave a certain way. This is where our ability to predict OOD behaviours will come from. Whilst we intend to build models that provide concrete numerical predictions of behaviour, we also want them to be usable as intuition pumps for informal reasoning too.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefa90h2f5zhjw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fna90h2f5zhjw">[1]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Cognitive</span></b><span>: our explanations will be given in terms of theoretical constructs—latent variables interpreted as mental states and processes (goals, beliefs, preferences, motivations)—sitting a layer below behaviour and above internals.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgw30doszts"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngw30doszts">[2]</a></sup></span><span> In our theories, these constructs pay rent through their ability to predict behaviour.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpr94hyhmnh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpr94hyhmnh">[3]</a></sup></span><span> We claim these constructs are the natural unit at which to reason about scheming, reward-seeking, and OOD generalisation.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Developmental</span></b><span>: we seek to model how these cognitive constructs evolve over the course of training, rather than reasoning about only those possessed at the end of training. Post-training objectives underspecify the agent’s behaviour across all domains, with pre-training and mid-training shaping motivations and steering how later training is generalised from. Additionally, by modelling the effects of each training stage, it becomes easier to compose the effects of multiple stages together. This allows us to predict cognition on training pipelines more complex than those tested.</span></li></ul><p><span>The agenda rests on four load-bearing assumptions, in increasing specificity:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>(A1)</span></b><span> The OOD behaviour of AIs is non-arbitrary and has some kind of structure, but this structure is not necessarily readily interpretable.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>(A2)</span></b><span> That structure can be captured by some interpretable latent variables that compress observations of an agent's behaviour (the cognitive construct).</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>(A3)</span></b><span> The evolution of these latents can be predicted with training information</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefoixohki8wvk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnoixohki8wvk">[4]</a></sup></span><span> alone (</span><i><span>i.e.</span></i><span>, without needing behavioural or mechanistic data from the trained model itself).</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>(A4)</span></b><span> The training-to-latents and latents-to-behaviour mappings can be learnt from observing similar AIs, and will generalise to training pipelines and deployment situations that are different to those that were observed.</span></li></ul><p><span>If successful, we would have predictive tools for evaluating the effects of complex training pipelines, and a much stronger general understanding of LLM cognition that would allow us to make progress on questions such as the likelihood and potential effectiveness of scheming and reward seeking. Even short of full success, identifying where behaviour resists prediction would itself help flag areas where guarantees of safety might be difficult to achieve even by other methods, useful for technical, policy, and advocacy work.</span></p><h1><span>3. Worked Example: RL agents trained to navigate a maze</span></h1><p><span>In this section, we demonstrate how we apply the ideas discussed above in a toy setting. For the full detail, see </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23565"><span>our paper</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>We trained CNN-based RL agents on tasks in which they had to navigate to a goal object within a maze. Goal objects each had a shape and a colour—for example, red diamonds or blue crosses. We train each agent on a pipeline consisting of first being trained to pursue one goal, and then a different one: for example, black plusses followed by red circles. We then attempted to predict the OOD behaviour of agents in a forced choice setting—specifically, we placed agents in mazes in which two goals (with different shape-colour combinations) were present, and measured their propensity to pursue one goal over another.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 57.78197320206287%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/52d3d08b263e23bf4204f46dba93ffa1d1ca2a39cf4a477a976baae3297a5bc4/pyeakrz0psltyku6xkq9" /></figure><p><span>How do the four assumptions laid out above apply to our case?</span></p><p><b><span>(A1) Structured OOD behaviour</span></b><span>. We found that, although the agents were only ever exposed to training environments with a single goal at a time, and only to two goals total out of a possible 24 colour-shape combinations, their OOD behaviour was coherent and had obvious structure. For example, agents trained on red diamonds would often pursue red-coloured objects in the forced-choice setting, and agents trained on blue crosses would often pursue cross-shaped objects.</span></p><p><b><span>(A2) Capturing this structure with latent cognitive constructs</span></b><span>. In this case, the OOD behaviour of each agent was well captured by a small set of </span><i><span>values </span></i><span>which predicted pairwise choice probabilities across all possible forced choices. Specifically, assigning a value to each colour-shape combination and using a Boltzmann-rational model of choice allow us to compress 276 forced-choice probabilities into a set of just 24 interpretable score values.</span></p><p><b><span>(A3) Predicting latent evolution with training information</span></b><span>. We develop a methodology for predicting how these score values will evolve over the course of training which we call </span><i><span>latent policy gradient</span></i><span>. For any given training pipeline, we are able to use LPG to predict the value scores that our RL agents will possess at the end of training.</span></p><p><b><span>(A4) Predicting unseen pipelines</span></b><span>. We further show that our method can predict the OOD behaviour of agents trained on held-out pipelines, by understanding the effects of individual pipelines.  </span></p><p><span>This paper uses relatively simple models of both cognition—Boltzmann rationality over score values—and development—our latent policy gradient method. However, we think this is an important proof-of-concept for the overall approach, and are excited to scale up our methods to more sophisticated models of cognition and development appropriate for LLMs.</span></p><h1><span>4. Why we expect this to scale to LLMs</span></h1><p><span>We developed and tested our methodology in a toy setting of CNN-based RL agents pursuing colour-shape combinations in mazes, and found that it worked effectively. Encouraged by our early results, we have some reasons to expect why this agenda should be fruitful when we turn our attention to LLMs. We recap the assumptions underpinning our agenda and evaluate to what extent we already have existing evidence for or against them.</span></p><p><b><span>(A1) Structured OOD behaviour.</span></b><span> This holds on many domains, and LLMs seem to have </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.15236"><span>identifiable values</span></a><span>, with their </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.09251"><span>systematic behavioural tendencies grow with training scale</span></a><span>. However, LLMs can also exhibit </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25891"><span>highly conditional behaviours</span></a><span> and are influenced by </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05910"><span>spurious correlations in post training</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>(A2) Capturing this structure with latent cognitive constructs.</span></b><span> This has been demonstrated across behavioural and mechanistic approaches. The values of LLMs seem amenable to modelling with </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/k6HKzwqCY4wKncRkM/brief-explorations-in-llm-value-rankings"><span>Boltzmann-rational</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13339"><span>Thurstonian</span></a><span> approaches, and we’re finding low-dimensional internal representations of cognitive phenomena such as </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/assistant-axis"><span>personas</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>(A3) Predicting latent evolution with training information, </span></b><span>and </span><b><span>(A4) predicting unseen pipelines.</span></b><span> These have not been directly demonstrated in LLMs, but there are results that provide evidence that they might hold. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2001.08361/1000"><span>Neural scaling</span></a><span> laws demonstrate that LLM next token prediction loss is itself easily predicted by training information, with broader training-data to behaviour relationships having </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.03296"><span>predictable structure across scales</span></a><span>. Alignment techniques inspired by cognitive-level reasoning seem effective both for </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10160"><span>pre-training</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/teaching-claude-why/"><span>mid-/post-training</span></a><span>, and the coherence of cognitive models of LLM capabilities and preferences </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08640"><span>increases with scale</span></a><span>. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.17424"><span>Initially surprising results</span></a><span> show consistency across </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11613"><span>model sizes, model families, and datasets</span></a><span>, and also seem to have </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.19823"><span>interpretable latent causes</span></a><span>. Indirect evidence aside, properly testing these assumptions is our next focus.</span></p><h1><span>5. Open problems &amp; call to engage</span></h1><p><span>There's lots of work to be done! Here are some research questions we're interested in, both ones that can be started upon immediately, and ones which are more long-term directions.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Whether Developmental Cognitive Interpretability can scale to LLMs at all.</span></b><span> We're actively working on applying latent policy gradients to simple character-training-style pipelines and seeing how they shape LLM behaviour on forced-choice value rankings. The hope is to recover a notion of personas in the form of how optimising for certain character traits and values are correlated with one another.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Exploring toy settings more thoroughly.</span></b><span> Testing how different architectures and RL algorithms generalise, testing more complicated training pipelines, and testing other toy settings.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Expanding the search space of cognitive models.</span></b><span> We've already done some follow-up on this in our toy setting, but we're hoping to find cases where our simplest models fail before resorting to more complex ones. We’re also excited to explore cognitive models with deeper structure, such as </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1"><span>motivational DAGs</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Applying DCI to informal cognitive theories</span></b><span> like the </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/dfoty34sT7CSKeJNn/the-persona-selection-model"><span>Persona Selection Model</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/the-behavioral-selection-model-for"><span>Behavioural Selection Model</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iCfdcxiyr2Kj8m8mT/the-shard-theory-of-human-values"><span>Shard Theory</span></a><span>. This allows us to formalise these theories, test them, and iterate on them. We have some ideas about what this might look like, but we will not be able to properly test them until we’ve validated the general approach works for LLM behaviour.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span>Exploring broader training paradigms than goal-based RL.</span></b><span> For example, RLHF, DPO, deliberative alignment, prompt-distillation, SDF, and AI debate. By coming up with methods to apply DCI in each of these paradigms, we give ourselves the building blocks to model complex training pipelines used for frontier AIs.</span></li><li value="6"><b><span>Using DCI to understand the effects of frontier training pipelines.</span></b><span> This is the main goal of the DCI agenda, and would require significant progress on all the previous open problems.</span></li></ul><p><span>If you find any of this interesting or promising, please get in touch! We think a lot of people are starting to have ideas in this broad direction, and it seems worth trying to co-ordinate this effectively. Jason will be at EAG London 2026 this weekend and would be glad to talk about any of this in person.</span></p><p><span>Finally, we’re also interested in any pushback and concerns people have about this research direction.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fna90h2f5zhjw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefa90h2f5zhjw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>To contrast, see </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.10938"><span>this paper</span></a><span> for an example of LLM behavioural modelling that is not interpretable.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngw30doszts"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgw30doszts">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.05107v1"><span>Marr's terms</span></a><span>, cognitive constructs sit at the computational/algorithmic level, whereas weights and activations sit at the implementational level.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpr94hyhmnh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpr94hyhmnh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Rather than, </span><i><span>e.g.</span></i><span>, by reducibility to </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.19823"><span>features</span></a><span> or </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.21509"><span>activation patterns</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnoixohki8wvk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefoixohki8wvk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>By this we mean all inputs to the training process, so this could include details of model architectures or optimisers in order to account for their inductive biases.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/oCcGiDzWYQeJkhhZY/developmental-cognitive-interpretability-a-research-agenda-1#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Trees are mostly made of air and a generalizable lesson for AI safety](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xiTBpBDwubnr4MLRe/trees-are-mostly-made-of-air-and-a-generalizable-lesson-for)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>At the risk of embarrassing myself, I’ll share a confession.</p><p>For context, I took five years of Latin: four in high school and one in college. In addition to learning the language, all my Latin classes taught a lot about Roman history. Emperors, internal politics, Caesar, etc. I was always learning some random bag of facts about Roman history. In high school, I won the award for top Latin student in my graduating class. So I wasn’t a bad Latin student.</p><p>Here’s the confession: I somehow don’t even vaguely remember the rough timespan the Roman Empire existed. Maybe Jesus time? I know he was killed by the Romans (is that right?). Were they around for a long time after? A long time before that? When was Romulus and Remus allegedly fighting? Virgil wrote the Aeneid when? I don’t have a clue. Despite being a kind of “Latin expert” I am missing a much more important foundational fact: when all of this was happening.</p><hr /><p>When I say trees are made out of air I’m not talking about the fact that there is a lot of empty space inside a tree (or actually anything made out of atoms). I mean something more mind-blowing.&nbsp;</p><p>Imagine you are holding a piece of dry wood. Where did that wood come from?&nbsp;<i>A tree.</i> Okay sure, but what are you actually holding? Where did the tree get that stuff? It turns out that almost all of the mass in dry wood comes from the&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;in the air. This follows from a simple fact about how photosynthesis works:</p><span class="math-tex"></span><p>The carbon and the oxygen in the glucose (<span class="math-tex"></span>) come from the&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and the hydrogen comes from the water. Since hydrogen is the lightest atom in the universe, it adds almost no mass, so nearly all the mass of glucose traces back to the&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>. Wood is mostly built from glucose, so by mass, a dead tree is mostly the carbon and oxygen that came out of the air.</p><p>It’s kind of unintuitive. Wood is hard and stiff and air is not even close to either of those things. But if you are a biologist or know some basic chemistry you would think that this is the kind of obvious thing you would want to know. At the very least you would expect the best math and science students in the world to be familiar with such basic biology. But take a look at this video of a documentary film crew asking MIT graduates where they think wood comes from:</p><figure class="media"><div><div></div></div></figure><p>One student, upon being told that most of the mass in the wood comes from&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;and not the dirt in the ground, said “that's very disturbing and I wonder how that could happen.” And that’s an MIT graduate.</p><p>“Trees come from mostly air” is pretty fundamental for biology because it follows from photosynthesis which is in many ways the basis for life on earth. In a sane world, every 8th grader would know that dry wood is mostly&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, not like minerals found in the ground or something. MIT grads probably have vast amounts of detailed knowledge of math, physics, biology, chemistry or all of the above. But they don’t know some basic facts about biology.&nbsp;</p><hr /><p>There is this assumption that you should first learn the foundational facts of an area of study and then move to more and more specific questions and ideas. As you move up levels of classes, the foundational stuff seems more and more basic and less and less relevant. Some stuff is continually hammered in because it’s useful background knowledge. In a perfect world, this helps you internalize the basics and learn how to reason about them to solve harder and harder problems.</p><p>There are some areas where, without much effort, this may work out. For example, you learn fractions in first or second grade but will probably understand them more deeply by the time you get to calculus because you need to know fractions to do calculus and all the classes that come before calculus. But not all foundational knowledge is like this! Knowing where trees come from doesn’t help you answer organic chemistry or evolutionary biology questions.&nbsp;</p><p>Being 1. foundational and being 2. useful for answering increasingly specific questions are different things. They are certainly not orthogonal but they are also not perfectly correlated. When 1 &amp; 2 diverge, you get MIT grads who are confused about what wood is.</p><hr /><p>In AI safety, this can be a serious problem.</p><p>I have had one-on-ones or interviewed dozens of students who want a career in AI safety. There are many examples of students who are something like this: they know what alignment faking is, read LessWrong, know who Neel Nanda is, know what METR is, have done an interp project, etc. But when you ask them why they care about AI safety they don’t provide a particularly coherent answer. So I get more specific: “Why should we think AI is an existential risk?” Again, incoherent answer.</p><p>This may be because they don’t really care much about AI safety and they just like to hang out with EA/rationalist types. An alternative reason (which I think is more likely) is because AI safety basics are something you learn and don’t exercise. When you do a BlueDot reading group you hopefully learn AI safety basics. But when doing interp experiments or your first SPAR research project, you think about specific empirical questions, not the orthogonality thesis. You don’t think about the basics and you don’t internalize them and certainly cannot reason about them.</p><p>Instrumental convergence, inner alignment, reward misspecification, etc. are our “trees are made out of air” or “the Roman Empire was 27 BC to 476 AD”. But lots of people in AI safety programs or those applying for them don’t know the basic facts. They know a lot of specific things but somehow not the foundational things.</p><span class="math-tex"></span><p>This strikes me as a genuine failure mode. A lot of focus goes into having great fellowship programs and university groups but some conceptual knowledge seems to be slipping through the cracks. I hope the next generation of AI safety researchers have all of the conceptual knowledge of earlier researchers and more.</p><hr /><p>I’ll leave you with an additional confession. 6ish years ago, before I started college, this was probably me, at least partly. I understood the basic alignment problem but understood it mostly through outer-alignment issues and didn’t fully internalize the difficulty of the problem until I started college. That was roughly 4 years ago when I moved into the UChicago dorms. From the very beginning, I was a CS major because I wanted to be an AI safety researcher. A few hours ago I turned in my last paper, and now I’m done.</p><p>My primary reflection is this: I would not have become a CS major, would not have worked so hard, would not have been so laser-focused on AI safety if I didn’t actually understand it. I would have got distracted and ended up probably on Wall Street or worse. This is because knowing a problem tells you why you should care. So if there is one reason to embrace the basics, it is that. There is so much fucking power in actually understanding something.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xiTBpBDwubnr4MLRe/trees-are-mostly-made-of-air-and-a-generalizable-lesson-for#comments">Discuss</a>
